# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. Two origins, one fusion, four phases

- ✅ Asian origin: Japan occupied Manchuria in **1931** and expanded into full-scale war in China from **1937**. European origin: Germany and the USSR occupied Poland in **September 1939**, divided on **29 September** under the pact of August 1939; Britain and France declared war on **3 September 1939**.
- ✅ Permissive conditions accumulated over eight years - Manchuria 1931, Abyssinia 1935, the Rhineland 1936, Munich 1938, Prague 1939 - and are owned by the interwar-diplomacy topic.
- ✅ Fusion within six months of 1941: **Barbarossa 22 June**, **Pearl Harbor 7 December**, and **Hitler's declaration of war on the USA**.
- ⚠️ The asymmetry to memorise: the Axis fought two separate wars; the Allies fought one.
- ✅ Four phases: opening moves Sept 1939 - Dec 1940; the Axis offensive widens 1941 - summer 1942; offensives held in check summer 1942 - summer 1943; the Axis defeated July 1943 - Aug 1945.
- ✅ The contrast with 1914-18: a war of rapid movement and a much more complex affair, fought in the Pacific and the Far East, North Africa, deep in Russia, central and western Europe and the Atlantic.
- ✅ Vocabulary: **Blitzkrieg** - rapid thrusts by motorised divisions and tanks supported by air power; the **phoney war**, named by the American press; the **Second Front**, demanded by the Russians from 1941; **unconditional surrender**; the **Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere**; the **Final Solution**, the perpetrators' own euphemism.

## 2. The opening campaigns and the four conditions of the method

- ⚠️ The method required a land frontier, dispersed or absent enemy armour, local air superiority and a short distance to the decisive objective.
- ✅ **Poland:** the Luftwaffe destroyed the Polish air force and railway system; no Polish motorised divisions; massed cavalry charges; Anglo-French inaction because French mobilisation was slow and out of date.
- ✅ **Phoney war:** five months in the west; the USSR took Estonia, Latvia and Lithuania and invaded Finland in November 1939; Maginot and Siegfried Lines manned; Hitler's generals doubted the army's readiness to attack in the west.
- ✅ **Norway, April 1940:** Narvik was the outlet for Swedish iron ore; Raeder wanted the fjords against Britain's transatlantic supply lines; the *Altmark* affair; landings on 9 April at Oslo, Kristiansand, Stavanger, Bergen and Trondheim; Norwegian troops not mobilised; Quisling's local Nazis gave every assistance; no British air support. **Two results:** three cruisers and ten destroyers lost, weakening the navy at Dunkirk; and Chamberlain's resignation, bringing Churchill to office with drive, urgency and the ability to make a coalition cabinet work.
- ✅ **10 May - 22 June 1940:** Holland surrendered in four days after the bombing of Rotterdam; Belgium at the end of May; Dunkirk 27 May - 4 June, over 338,000 evacuated, two-thirds British, made possible by Hitler's halt order of 24 May; all arms and equipment lost; Paris 14 June; surrender 22 June; the armistice at Compiegne in the 1918 railway coach; northern France and the Atlantic coast occupied, giving submarine bases; Petain's unoccupied France collaborated; Halifax explored German terms through Washington and even Churchill considered a negotiated peace.
- ✅ **Four causes of the French defeat:** political division with a peace party under Laval and communist opposition after the pact; structural weakness, with the undivided German weight and a Maginot Line not continued along the Belgian frontier because Petain believed the Ardennes barrier enough; doctrinal error, with as many tanks as Germany but dispersed among infantry divisions and no combat-plane support; and command failures, including no attack in the west in September 1939 and no troops moved from inactive Maginot forts to block the Meuse breakthrough of 13 May 1940. ✅ Petain, hero of Verdun in 1916, urged peace; Reynaud resigned.

## 3. The Battle of Britain and the definition of a turning point

- ✅ Fought in the air **12 August - 30 September 1940**; Goering's Luftwaffe aimed to destroy the RAF as the preliminary to invasion; targets in order were harbours, radar stations, aerodromes and munitions factories, with London from September, claimed as retaliation for a British raid on Berlin.
- ✅ Losses: **1,389 German aircraft against 792 British**; Hitler cancelled the invasion when British air power proved not to have been destroyed.
- ✅ Four reasons: the radar chain; poorly armed German bombers; German fighters limited to about **ninety minutes** in the air, the British fighters being *not significantly better* than the Messerschmitts; and the switch to London, which relieved the airfields at the critical moment.
- ✅ Described as **probably the first major turning point**: the Germans checked for the first time and shown not to be invincible; Britain remained in the struggle, so Hitler faced war on two fronts.
- ⚠️ Definition carried through the session: a turning point removes an option on which the loser's strategy depended; it is not identical with final victory.
- ⚠️ Two lists, and the clause that separates them: the **strict five** - Battle of Britain, Midway, El Alamein, Stalingrad, the Atlantic - are the Axis defeats that pass that test; the owner's turning-point section adds **D-Day** as a sixth and its evidence bank adds **Barbarossa** and **Pearl Harbor** for eight. Use the wider list for a *discuss* stem and the strict five for a *weigh or rank* stem, and say which. See Lesson 3 section 4, Lesson 8 section 7, Lesson 20 rehearsal item 2 and the footer of Map 4.

## 4. The Mediterranean and the Balkan diversion

- ✅ September 1940: Mussolini advanced about **sixty miles** into Egypt from Libya; October: another army invaded Greece from Albania.
- ✅ Britain drove the Italians out of Egypt and deep into Libya, winning at **Beda Fomm** with **130,000 prisoners and 400 tanks**; naval aircraft sank **half the Italian fleet at Taranto** in November 1940; five more warships were destroyed off **Cape Matapan** in March 1941; the Greeks pushed the Italians back into Albania.
- ✅ **February 1941:** Rommel and the Afrika Korps to Tripoli; by June 1942 the Germans were approaching El Alamein, **seventy miles from Alexandria**.
- ✅ **April 1941:** Germany invaded Greece the day after 60,000 British, Australian and New Zealand troops arrived; Athens fell; Crete taken by parachute assault in May; Yugoslavia invaded simultaneously.
- ✅ **Three effects of the Greek campaign:** Allied losses; troops stripped from North Africa when most needed against Rommel; and a **five-week delay** to Barbarossa, originally planned for **15 May** - with the recorded conditional that an attack begun then *might well* have captured Moscow before winter.
- ✅ The desert war was a serious drain on German resources needed in Russia; ⚠️ Italy subtracted German capability rather than adding Italian capability.

## 5. Barbarossa: the decision and the assumption

- ✅ **22 June 1941**, breaking a non-aggression pact signed less than two years earlier.
- ✅ **Four motives:** fear of a Soviet attack while forces were occupied in the west; hope that Japan would attack Russia in the Far East; the belief that a stronger Japan would keep the USA out; and above all hatred of communism and the desire for *Lebensraum*.
- ✅ **Bullock:** Hitler invaded for the simple and sufficient reason that he had always meant to annex the territory between the Vistula and the Urals. ✅ **Trevor-Roper:** the campaign was not a luxury but the be-all and end-all of Nazism; it could not be delayed; it was now or never. ✅ Hitler: kick in the door and the whole rotten structure will come crashing down.
- ✅ **Scale:** close on **5.5 million men**, **3,550 tanks**, **5,000 aircraft**, **47,000 pieces of artillery**; three prongs - north to Leningrad, centre to Moscow, south through the Ukraine; Riga, Smolensk and Kiev taken; Leningrad and Moscow not.
- ✅ Soviet unpreparedness: British and American warnings discounted; Stalin trusted the pact and distrusted the source; the army was re-equipping and many generals inexperienced after the purges.
- ✅ October rains turned the roads to mud; November-December frosts reached **minus 38 degrees Celsius** in places; **winter clothing was inadequate because the campaign was expected to end by autumn**; in spring 1942 Hitler drove south-east for the Caucasus oilfields.
- ⚠️ The formulation: the army was defeated not by the winter but by a planning assumption that made the winter fatal.

## 6. December 1941 and the arithmetic

- ✅ The chain: American assistance to China; Japan obtains French Indo-China by persuading **Vichy France**; Roosevelt demands withdrawal and imposes an **oil embargo on 26 July 1941**; stalemate over withdrawal from China itself; **Tojo becomes prime minister on 16 October**.
- ✅ **Pearl Harbor, 7 December 1941:** organised by **Yamamoto**; no declaration of war; **353 planes** arrived undetected and destroyed **350 aircraft and five battleships** in two hours; Roosevelt's "a date which will live in infamy".
- ✅ **Two results:** control of the Pacific, with Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong, Burma, the Dutch East Indies, the Philippines, Guam and Wake taken by May 1942; and Hitler's declaration of war on the USA.
- ✅ **Three reasons for the declaration:** an assurance already given to Japan; the assumption that Roosevelt would declare war eventually; and the wish to get in first, to show the German people that he controlled events. ✅ Congress wanted revenge on Japan and was still reluctant about Europe; Hitler's action saved Roosevelt the trouble.
- ✅ Germany now faced the immense potential of the USA, with the USSR and the British Commonwealth: the longer the war lasted, the less chance of an Axis victory; swift knock-out blows were essential.
- ⚠️ Barbarossa made the war unendable; December 1941 made it unwinnable.

## 7. Occupation, resistance and collaboration

- ✅ Both occupations were ruthless and brutal. The Nazis treated the peoples of eastern Europe as **sub-humans fit only to be slaves** of a master race; the Jews were to be exterminated. ✅ Shirer: millions driven into **forced labour**, millions tortured in the camps.
- ✅ **The verdict: amoral and foolish** - in the Baltic states and the Ukraine, Soviet rule was so unpopular that decent treatment would have turned the people into allies.
- ✅ Japan treated prisoners of war and Asian peoples badly; many Asians, those of **Indo-China** among them, at first welcomed them as liberators from European control; the **Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere** was to be defended by sea and air power; harsh treatment turned them against Tokyo and produced determined resistance movements, usually with communist involvement.
- ✅ **Named anchors and no others:** **Vidkun Quisling** and the Norwegian local Nazis; **Vichy France**, a collaborationist authority with its own colonies and armed forces, technically on the Axis side, which permitted the Japanese occupation of Indo-China; and the **Italian partisans** who captured and shot Mussolini and displayed his body in Milan.
- ✅ The wartime-to-postwar link: in **Malaya**, Chinese communist guerrillas led by **Chin Peng**, who had led the anti-Japanese resistance, turned on the British; the emergency declared in **1948** remained in force until **1960**.
- ✅ Nazi racial policy is listed among the reasons the Axis lost; the advanced owner lists occupation brutality among six such factors.
- ⚠️ Resistance and collaboration are two answers to one question put to the same societies; grading nations is an error of fact.

## 8. The three checks and the Atlantic

- ✅ **Midway, June 1942:** five Japanese carriers, nearly 400 aircraft, seventeen large warships and a 5,000-strong invasion force against three American carriers and 233 planes; **four carriers and about 330 planes** destroyed. Reasons: the **broken Japanese radio code**; the splitting of Japanese forces; the simultaneous launch from all four carriers, leaving the fleet vulnerable while rearming; and dive-bombers attacking from **19,000 feet**. ✅ Japan's far more numerous battleships and cruisers were mostly ineffective: only carrier air power could decide the Pacific. ✅ Island hopping under MacArthur began with the Solomon Islands in August 1942.
- ✅ **El Alamein, October 1942:** the culmination of the July check and Alam Halfa in September; **80,000 men and 540 tanks against 230,000 troops and 1,440 tanks** after a seven-week pause; Allied air power sank Axis supply ships, producing shortages of food, fuel oil and ammunition; Montgomery's preparations probably clinched it, though he was criticised for over-caution and for allowing Rommel and half his force to escape. **Three consequences:** Egypt and the Suez Canal saved; no Axis link-up with the forces in the Ukraine; and expulsion from North Africa, with **275,000** surrendering in Tunisia in May 1943.
- ✅ **Stalingrad:** reached at the end of August 1942; the city more or less destroyed; the Russians refused to surrender; a November pincer against dangerously extended supply lines; **von Paulus surrendered with 94,000 men on 2 February 1943**. Had it fallen, the Caucasus oil route would have been cut and an advance up the Don against Moscow was planned. It shattered the invincibility myth; **Kursk** followed in July 1943; the siege of Leningrad was abandoned early in 1944.
- ✅ **The Atlantic:** the German bases came from the occupation of the French Atlantic coast in June 1940; the British navy's early record was mixed because air support was neglected and carriers few, producing defeats in Norway and Crete. Successes: **Taranto** (Nov 1940, from the carrier *Illustrious*), **Cape Matapan** (Mar 1941), the ***Bismarck*** (May 1941), the Crete invasion transports (May 1941). **Arctic convoys** from September 1941: the first twelve unmolested, convoy 17 losing twenty-three of thirty-six ships in June 1942, suspension until November 1943, and **forty convoys in all with 720 of 811 ships arriving**, carrying **5,000 tanks and 7,000 aircraft**.
- ✅ **The U-boat series:** **ninety U-boats operating and 250 building** at the start of 1942; **over four million tons lost and twenty-one U-boats destroyed** in six months; a peak of **108 ships in March 1943**, almost two-thirds in convoy; the **July 1943 crossover**. Three reasons: long-range **Liberators**; escorts and aircraft improving with experience; and **centimetric radar** small enough for aircraft, detecting submarines in poor visibility and at night. ✅ **Doenitz** complained to Hitler in May 1943 that more U-boats were being destroyed by aircraft than by naval vessels. ✅ The verdict: as important as Midway, El Alamein and Stalingrad, because Britain could not have sustained the March 1943 losses and stayed in the war.

## 9. Air power and the bombing controversy

- ✅ Five achievements: the Battle of Britain; naval co-operation including Taranto, Cape Matapan and the sinking of the ***Tirpitz*** in Norway in November 1943; the Pacific carrier war, island hopping and air supply in the Burma campaign; desert support flown from Egypt and Palestine against Rommel's supply ships and armies; and airborne operations at Sicily in July 1943 and Normandy in June 1944, with **Arnhem in September 1944 as the failure**.
- ✅ The **strategic air offensive**: massive attacks on military and industrial targets; the Ruhr, Cologne, Hamburg and Berlin badly hit; some raids seem to have been carried out to undermine civilian morale, as at Dresden in February 1945; raids on Japan from the Marianas from early 1945, with a quarter of Tokyo destroyed in March.
- ✅ **Seven named positions:** **Lindqvist** (2001) - systematic attacks on German civilians in their homes should be seen as crimes under international humanitarian law; **Neillands** (2001) - in total war this was the necessary price of peace; **Friedrich**, *Der Brand* (2002; English 2007) - war crimes, blaming Churchill and Arthur "Bomber" Harris; **Barnett** - a historical travesty designed to shift the spotlight from Nazi atrocities; **Overy**, in *History Today*, January 2007 - time for a proper assessment, Friedrich plays down the American contribution, the tone is unhelpful, and the bombing was neither immoral nor strategically useless; **Tooze** - if you start a war with Britain as Germany deliberately did, this is the kind of war you have to be prepared to fight; **Kent** - probably war crimes, but the Nazis bombed civilians first at **Guernica, Warsaw and Rotterdam**.
- ✅ **Effectiveness:** German archives show the RAF attack on the **Ruhr in spring 1943** had an immediate effect on production; from **July 1944**, with greater accuracy and the new **Mustang** escorts which could outmanoeuvre all German fighters, **synthetic oil production fell rapidly**; in **October 1944 the Krupp works at Essen** were permanently out of action; the war effort ground to a halt in 1945 and Japan was in the same state by June 1945.
- ✅ **The verdict:** after much wasted effort early on, the offensive was **one of the decisive reasons for the Axis defeat**, strangling fuel and armaments production, destroying railway communications, and **diverting many aircraft from the eastern front**, which helped the Russian advance. ✅ The advanced owner's three positions: morally indefensible; a grim necessity of total war; and wasteful early but decisively effective by 1944-45.
- ⚠️ No bombing casualty figure is used anywhere in this session.

## 10. Alliance, grand strategy and the war economies

- ✅ **Lend-Lease Act signed 11 March 1941**; the **Atlantic Charter of 14 August 1941** - both before American belligerency.
- ✅ **Unconditional surrender** announced at **Casablanca, January 1943**; **Goebbels** recorded that he could never have thought up so rousing a slogan; **Eisenhower** and many leading Americans opposed it as likely to prolong the war; the chiefs of staff pressed Roosevelt before D-Day and he refused, in case it was taken as a sign of weakness; **Truman** continued it; no negotiations until both surrendered. ✅ **Fleming**, *History Today*, December 2001: an ultimatum written in blood.
- ✅ Conference sequence: **Casablanca January 1943**, **Tehran 28 November - 1 December 1943**, **Yalta 4-11 February 1945**, **Potsdam 17 July - 2 August 1945**. ⚠️ The postwar content of the last two belongs to the Cold War owner.
- ✅ The Russians demanded the Second Front from 1941; the recorded preconditions for the landing were the elimination of Italy, control of the U-boats and Allied air superiority.
- ✅ **Raw materials:** Italy and Japan had to import; Germany was short of rubber, cotton, nickel and, after mid-1944, oil. ✅ **The qualifier: the shortages need not have been fatal, but success depended on a swift end to the war**; Britain's survival in 1940 kept the western front alive until the USA entered.
- ✅ Soviet industry moved **east of the Urals**; by 1945 **four times as many tanks** as the Germans and **twice as many men** in the field. ✅ American peak output: **over 70,000 tanks and 120,000 aircraft a year**.
- ✅ **Speer** appeared to have worked a miracle; **Tooze** has shown he was more successful as a self-publicist, claiming credit for earlier policies, blaming others for failures and producing **a stream of false statistics** to the end.
- ✅ Allied learning: by 1942 they knew how to check Blitzkrieg and valued air support and carriers. ✅ Axis failures: Japanese battleship construction; **V-rockets instead of jet aircraft**, which might well have restored German air superiority.
- ❌ No output series, sectoral breakdown, labour or national-income data is held here, and none is supplied.

## 11. The Holocaust: sequence, mechanism and historiography

- ✅ **Majdanek** near Lublin found by Soviet forces at the end of **July 1944**, with seven gas chambers; the victims there included Soviet prisoners of war and Poles as well as a Jewish majority. ✅ At least **twenty camps** were set up for what the regime called the **Final Solution** - *Endlosung*.
- ✅ **The single attributed estimate used here:** between **December 1941**, when the first Jews were killed at **Chelmno**, and **May 1945**, **some 5.7 million Jews were murdered**, along with hundreds of thousands of non-Jews - **gypsies, socialists, communists, homosexuals and people with mental disabilities**, named separately. ⚠️ Estimates vary; the figure is attributed and no other is given.
- ✅ **Intentionalists:** responsibility rests on Hitler, who planned extermination from his accession; *Mein Kampf*; the **Reichstag speech of January 1939** prophesying the annihilation of the Jewish race in Europe; **Bracher** - no master plan, but he knew what he wanted, and it was merely a matter of time and opportunity. ✅ **The objection:** why the nine-year delay, and why did Hitler order restraint after **Kristallnacht, November 1938**?
- ✅ **Functionalists:** the war aggravated the problem - about **three million Jews in Poland** from autumn 1939, several million more in **Belorussia and Ukraine** from June 1941, and sheer pressure of numbers leading local Nazi and SS leaders to press for mass murder; **Mommsen** - Hitler was "a weak dictator", and as late as 2001 no clear evidence of a genocidal bent before 1939.
- ✅ **Kershaw (2000):** personalised rule invited radical initiatives from below and backed them so long as they matched broadly defined goals; advancement meant anticipating the wish - **"working towards the Fuhrer"**. ✅ The reply to the absolution objection: the initiatives would not have been proposed but for awareness of his will.
- ✅ **Bullock (1991):** the most obvious interpretation combines both. ✅ **Overy (2004):** both divert attention from the central reality - the regime's vengeful and violent xenophobia had the Jews as its primary object throughout the whole life of the dictatorship.
- ✅ **Motive:** the secret **1936 memorandum**; communism seen as a Jewish phenomenon; German national survival against a worldwide conspiracy; Overy on the distorted mirror of German national anxieties.
- ✅ **Sequence:** the **Nuremberg Laws of 1935** to satisfy hardliners; **Kristallnacht November 1938** and the return to non-violence when opinion reacted unfavourably; exclusion, emigration and asset seizure, with **over half a million** leaving before the war; the **Madagascar** plan; the war and especially the invasion of Russia changing the situation; about **two and a half million** Polish Jews moved into ghettos in Warsaw, Lublin and Lodz; **Warsaw 375,000 in 1939** rising to about **445,000 by July 1941**, with food deliberately kept short.
- ✅ **December 1941:** Hitler publicly restated the 1939 prophecy; **Goebbels's diary** the next day - the World War is here, the extermination of the Jews must be the necessary consequence. ✅ The decision arguably taken in **autumn 1941**, on three recorded conditions: confidence after early Barbarossa successes; the ideological war of destruction in the east, in Bullock's phrase a racist-imperialist adventure in which the conventional rules were to be disregarded; and the possibility of acting outside Germany under strict censorship.
- ✅ **Wannsee, January 1942:** the logistics of removing **up to eleven million** Jews; Hitler did not attend and **no signed order was ever found**; **Kershaw's verdict** - his role was decisive and indispensable, and without him and the regime he headed such a programme would have been unthinkable.
- ✅ **Camps:** **Belzec, Sobibor, Treblinka and Majdanek** for eastern Europe, **Auschwitz-Birkenau** for most from western Europe; the **Warsaw ghetto rising of April 1943**, brutally crushed; Hungarian Jews taken to Auschwitz from **July 1944**; SS forced marches as Soviet forces advanced.
- ✅ **Perpetrators:** the SS, security police, camp commandants and guards, **gauleiters**, much of the **Wehrmacht**, businesses using camp labour, recipients of confiscated property, medical experts; **local militias in Lithuania** within three days of the invasion; **Antonescu's Romania** acting on its own initiative in a country never occupied. ✅ **And:** many Germans risked their lives to shelter Jews and organise escape routes, and often ended in camps themselves.
- ✅ **Goldhagen (1997)** and **Burleigh (2010)** hold a collective-responsibility thesis; the account holds that Goldhagen perhaps goes too far while affirming that large numbers of ordinary Germans went along with the regime. ⚠️ The thesis is reported, not adopted.
- ⚠️ **The decisive analytical point:** the programme continued, on Hitler's insistence, after the war had visibly been lost.

## 12. Total war and society

- ✅ **Four components:** massive civilian bombing; industry, transport and food supply made military targets; whole societies mobilised; and occupation and racial policy producing systematic atrocity. ✅ The war therefore belongs in the syllabus as the history of society under extreme state violence.
- ⚠️ **The definition is by targeting, not scale.** ⚠️ Two mechanisms: instrumental, against the capacity to fight; and constitutive, where the population is itself the object.
- ✅ Forced labour and displacement were primary features. ✅ After the war many millions were forced to move - worst in the areas taken from Germany by Russia and Poland and in German-speaking areas of Hungary, Romania and Czechoslovakia - with about **ten million Germans** moved west so that no future German government could claim those territories. ✅ Extensive urban redevelopment followed in the USSR and Germany.
- ✅ **Women:** directly recorded here among those deported into forced labour and among the victims of occupation atrocity. ⚠️ The sourced mechanism, taken from the First World War treatment, is that total war repeatedly enlarged women's economic role faster than it enlarged their political rights. ❌ No participation, wage, occupational or post-war status data for 1939-45 is held, and none is supplied.
- ✅ **The Beveridge Report, 1942** - a plan for introducing a welfare state, drafted during the war.
- ⚠️ **The comparison with 1914-18:** five theatres instead of one; movement instead of position; civilians targeted rather than affected; racial reordering as a war aim in one case, which is why this war produced genocide and the earlier one did not.

## 13. India and the colonial world at war

- ✅ **September 1939:** Britain declared India a belligerent without consulting Indian leaders; the **Congress ministries resigned**.
- ✅ **August 1940:** Linlithgow's **August Offer** - dominion status as a goal, an enlarged Executive Council, minority protection - rejected. ✅ **October 1940:** **Individual Satyagraha**, with **Vinoba Bhave** first and **Nehru** second.
- ✅ **1941:** after the Nazi attack on the USSR the **Communists adopted the People's War line**; the Muslim League stayed aloof from Quit India.
- ✅ **March-April 1942:** the **Cripps Mission** - post-war dominion status, a constitution-making body, provincial non-accession, princely states through their rulers; rejected; Gandhi's **"post-dated cheque"**.
- ✅ **8 August 1942:** **Quit India** adopted at **Gowalia Tank**, Bombay; **"Do or Die"**; leaders arrested on 9 August; parallel governments at **Ballia**, **Tamluk** (Jatiya Sarkar) and **Satara** (Prati Sarkar); underground leadership including **Jayaprakash Narayan, Ram Manohar Lohia, Aruna Asaf Ali and Usha Mehta**.
- ✅ **INA:** raised from prisoners of war under **Mohan Singh and Rash Behari Bose**; revived by **Subhas Chandra Bose** in **1943** with the **Provisional Government of Azad Hind** at Singapore; the **Rani of Jhansi Regiment**; the Andamans renamed **Shaheed and Swaraj**; failure at **Imphal-Kohima 1944**; the **Red Fort trials of 1945-46** of **Shah Nawaz Khan, Prem Sahgal and Gurbaksh Singh Dhillon** producing cross-communal sympathy; Bose officially reported to have died in an air crash in August 1945.
- ✅ **Simla Conference, 25 June - 14 July 1945**, convened by **Wavell** who had replaced Linlithgow in 1943; collapsed over the League's claim to nominate all Muslim members. ✅ **RIN ratings' revolt, February 1946**, at **HMIS Talwar**, Bombay - serious but short-lived and urban-centred.
- ✅ The general mechanism: Japanese defeats of Britain, Holland and France destroyed the tradition of European superiority; peoples who fought to expel the Japanese would not willingly return to European rule.
- ❌ The Bengal famine of 1943 is not held in this evidence base and nothing is supplied about it here.

## 14. The endgame

- ✅ **Sicily, 10 July 1943**; the island quickly taken; **Mussolini dismissed by the king**; Salerno, Reggio and Taranto; **Naples October 1943**; **Badoglio's armistice** brought Italy in on the Allied side; the Germans rushed troops through the **Brenner Pass** to hold Rome and the north; **Anzio January 1944**; **Monte Cassino May**; **Rome June**; **Milan April 1945**. ✅ Contributions: air bases for bombing Central Europe and the Balkans, and German troops tied down when needed against Russia.
- ✅ **Overlord, 6 June 1944:** a **sixty-mile** Normandy front between Cherbourg and Le Havre - **Utah, Omaha, Gold, Juno, Sword**; **326,000 men** ashore in the first week; prefabricated **Mulberry harbours** towed from Britain.
- ✅ **Arnhem, September 1944** failed, though it might have worked had the troops landed nearer the two Rhine bridges. ✅ **Montgomery** wanted a rapid thrust to Berlin before the Russians; **Eisenhower** preferred a cautious broad front and prevailed, dispersing troops over **600 miles**.
- ✅ **The Ardennes, December 1944:** an offensive towards Antwerp through a weakly defended sector; a **sixty-mile** advance and a huge bulge; pushed back to the original position; important because Hitler had risked everything and lost men and tanks that could not be replaced.
- ✅ **Berlin fell to Stalin's forces in April 1945**; **Hitler committed suicide on 30 April**; the surrender was **signed at Reims on 7 May and ratified in Berlin on 8 May**.
- ✅ **Why Germany fought on:** **Tooze** - a large section of society was completely committed and generated the initiatives that made fighting to the death possible; **Kershaw** - the nature of the regime and Hitler's belief that relations between states were a life-and-death struggle admitting no compromise; Germans afraid of the enemy, especially the Russians, and of Nazi officials who hanged or shot those they called defeatists, deserters and cowards; and Kershaw's conclusion that terror was vital, as it had been before 1939. ✅ The post-war German appeal to Allied unconditional surrender is recorded and rejected: the explanation lies inside Germany.

## 15. The defeat of Japan

- ✅ Island hopping from the **Solomon Islands, August 1942**, under MacArthur, through 1943 and 1944, with heavy bombers preparing landings at the **Marianas (1944)** and the **Philippines (1945)**; raids on Japan from the Marianas from early 1945; **by June 1945 Japan had been reduced to the same state as Germany**.
- ✅ **6 August 1945** Hiroshima; **9 August** Nagasaki; the Japanese government surrendered. ✅ **8 August** the USSR declared war; **9 August** it invaded Manchuria. ✅ Surrender announced **15 August 1945**; signed **2 September 1945** aboard the USS *Missouri*.
- ✅ **Truman's justification:** saving American lives, since the war might otherwise have dragged on another year.
- ✅ **Three recorded counter-arguments** - on **necessity**: Japan had already put out peace feelers in July via Russia; on **motive**: to end the fighting before the Russians gained enough territory to share the occupation; on **purpose**: a deliberate demonstration to the USSR of American power.
- ✅ **The rule: never present a single cause.** Historians continue to debate the relative weight of the bombs, Soviet intervention, blockade, conventional destruction and the emperor-system question. ❌ No casualty figure is given for either city.

## 16. Why the Axis lost, and what the war changed

- ✅ **Five recorded reasons plus a sixth:** shortage of raw materials; Allied learning; the Axis taking on too much; the overwhelming combined resources of the USA, the USSR and the British Empire; tactical mistakes; and Nazi racial policy.
- ✅ **Liddell-Hart on Japan:** stretched far beyond their basic capacity for holding their gains, a small island state with limited industrial power. ✅ **Mussolini's incompetence was a constant drain on Hitler's resources.**
- ✅ **The five tactical mistakes:** battleships over carriers; failing to defeat Britain first; no provision for a winter campaign, with the no-retreat obsession that caused disasters at Stalingrad and left the troops exposed in Normandy; declaring war on the USA; and V-rockets over jet aircraft.
- ⚠️ **Ranking by function:** the **precondition** is Britain's survival; the **two multipliers** are Barbarossa and the American declaration; the **decisive medium** is combined Allied resources once the war was long; the **two accelerators** are the learning asymmetry and Allied sea and air superiority; the **subtractor** is occupation and racial policy.
- ✅ **Consequences:** enormous destruction, with **over thirty per cent of Italian national wealth** lost and heavy damage in Japan; **no all-inclusive peace settlement**, because distrust between the USSR and the west had re-emerged in the final months, leaving separate treaties - Italy's African colonies and her claims to Albania and Abyssinia surrendered; Soviet gains from Finland and eastern Czechoslovakia and retention of the Baltic states; Romania's recovery of northern Transylvania; **Trieste** a free territory protected by the UNO; **Japan at San Francisco in 1951** surrendering ninety years of acquisitions including a complete withdrawal from China; and **no settlement over Germany and Austria beyond occupation**, with **East Prussia divided between Russia and Poland**.
- ✅ **The nuclear age**, with the deterrent argument recorded as a position held by some. ✅ **The end of European primacy**, with Britain's **overseas debts of over £3,000 million**, investments sold, exports reduced, American aid abruptly stopped by Truman and a further high-interest loan producing uncomfortable dependence. ✅ **Two superpowers** with asymmetric strengths and immediate mutual suspicion. ✅ **Decolonisation** accelerated, with the **Algiers conference of 1973** and the **Third World** self-designation. ✅ **The United Nations Organization** as successor to the League.
- ⚠️ **Sort consequences into caused, accelerated and revealed**, and remember that the war accelerated decolonisation without completing it.

## 17. The answer architecture

- ✅ **Four theses:** origins - two regional wars fused by the decisions of 1941; defeat - operational brilliance could not compensate for overreach, weaker resources and self-defeating occupation; consequences - Europe disqualified from primacy, two non-European-centred superpowers, and the moral legitimacy of empire and racial hierarchy made unsustainable; genocide - a state project pursued through the war, to be analysed as policy rather than as wartime excess.
- ✅ **Mark-scaled spines:** **10** - thesis, two turning points or two reasons, one consequence, verdict; **15** - thesis, phases, turning points, why the Axis lost, effects, verdict; **20** - all of the above plus total war and the Holocaust, resistance and collaboration, and boundary and institutional consequences.
- ✅ **Verdict scaffolds:** on defeat, cumulative rather than at any one point; on transformation, a bipolar system replacing a European one, mass atrocity made a matter of international law, and colonial rule converted from a costly asset into an untenable one; on total war, not scale but targeting.
- ✅ **The nine-item risk-control list**, of which the three concerning numbers are one habit: when a figure would be emphasis rather than evidence, state the character of the event, attribute what is contested, and argue about causation.
- ⚠️ **Date controls for close-option distinctions:** 1931 and 1937 against September 1939; 1940 against 1941; 22 June against 7 December 1941; June 1942, October 1942 and 2 February 1943; 6 June 1944 against December 1944; May 1945 against August 1945; and the Nuremberg Laws of 1935 against the Nuremberg tribunal of 1945-46.

## Construction record for this edition

- ⚠️ The lesson count of twenty is derived from the dependency audit rather than from any source's chapter structure: two foundation lessons on definition and the opening campaigns, seven core lessons on the theatres and the checks, five advanced lessons on the instruments and the conduct of the war, one core lesson on the colonial dimension, two on the endgame, one on the explanation of defeat and two synthesis lessons.
- ⚠️ Structural variation is content-led. Lesson 1 is a definitional and boundary map; Lesson 2 is one machine run three times; Lesson 3 anatomises a single engagement as a broken causal chain; Lesson 4 is a single-road seesaw; Lesson 5 is a decision anatomy with rival historiographical readings; Lesson 6 is a demand chain with an arithmetic consequence; Lesson 7 is a two-hemisphere symmetry; Lesson 8 is three parallel mechanisms; Lesson 9 is the only lesson built on a numerical series; Lesson 10 is a six-position controversy sorted into two questions; Lesson 11 is a staged assembly; Lesson 12 is four balance sheets; Lesson 13 is an argument tree with criticisms attached to each branch; Lesson 14 is a definitional separation with a declared evidentiary gap; Lesson 15 is a two-clock comparison; Lesson 16 is a closing rail with one reversal; Lesson 17 is four candidate causes with a rule against choosing; Lesson 18 is a five-level ranking by function; Lesson 19 is a consequence fan sorted by kind of causation; Lesson 20 is a decision procedure.
- ⚠️ **No casualty total appears anywhere in this file**, for any battle, campaign, bombing or population, with the single exception of the attributed estimate for the murder of Europe's Jews stated once in Lesson 13 and repeated once in register note 11. The core source supplies several such figures and they are deliberately not reproduced, under the canonical owner's explicit control.
- ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to governments, high commands, ministries, SS units, police organisations, party formations, industrial firms and named individuals throughout. The one collective formulation in the evidence base is reported as its author's recorded position, examined and not adopted.
- ⚠️ One current linkage is used. It is taught and explicitly limited in Lesson 19 section 8, is truthfully recorded in that lesson's pre-teach checklist, and is recalled once as **Lesson 19 revision-note item 13**. It is recorded twice more inside this ledger - in the live-source row and in the source-manifest gate - as provenance rather than as teaching. That is five occurrences in all: one teaching use, one checklist entry, one revision recall and two ledger records. It appears nowhere else, is not extended, and no other lesson carries a current linkage.
- ⚠️ No compiled objective-question corpus, key, rotation or option-elimination material appears anywhere in this file.
- ⚠️ Three extraction limitations are disclosed at the point of use rather than filled: in Lesson 11, in Lesson 16 and in Lesson 19.
- ⚠️ Two open gaps are recorded rather than filled: the absence of women's war-work data for 1939-45 in Lesson 14, and the absence of any treatment of the Bengal famine of 1943 in Lesson 15.

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The dates of the Battle of Britain.** The core account's summary of events gives July to September 1940, while its detailed section gives 12 August to 30 September 1940; the canonical owner's mini-timeline follows the summary with July-Sept 1940 | The detailed section is preferred in teaching, because it is the section that discusses the battle, and the date range is given as 12 August to 30 September 1940. ⚠️ The discrepancy is recorded here rather than silently resolved, and no attempt is made to reconcile the two ranges |
| **The estimate for the murder of Europe's Jews.** The chapter's dedicated section gives some 5.7 million; its effects section gives "over five million"; the map caption in the same chapter gives a different total; and a quoted passage from a third writer uses "nearly six million" | The canonical owner's instruction is followed exactly: only the 5.7 million figure is used, it is attributed, and the variation among estimates is stated wherever it appears. ⚠️ The existence of four different formulations within one chapter is itself recorded here as evidence for why attribution is required |
| **The rendering of names in the extraction.** The OCR renders Beda Fomm as a single word, Alam Halfa with a transposed letter, and Lodz with a terminal letter that is not the standard one; the chapter's body text and its further-reading list also spell the surnames of the Swedish writer on bombing and of the historian who defended the offensive differently from each other | The standard forms are used in teaching - Beda Fomm, Alam Halfa, Lodz, Lindqvist and Neillands - and the variant renderings are recorded here. ⚠️ No factual claim depends on the difference, and no position is attributed to a differently spelled writer |
| **Whether the Balkan diversion delayed Barbarossa.** The chapter states that the involvement in Greece and Yugoslavia *may well have* delayed the attack and that an earlier start *might well have* captured Moscow before winter, while also recording structural reasons - mud, frost and the absence of winter clothing - that are independent of the start date | Both are taught, and the conditional wording is preserved verbatim in substance. ⚠️ Lesson 4 section 6 states explicitly that the delay argument concerns the margin and not the outcome |

## The single bounded material-fact pass

⚠️ One pre-declared verification pass was conducted on 29 September 2026, limited to a finite enumerated list of standard connecting names and dates whose omission would have created a real syllabus gap because neither canonical owner nor the core account supplies them. ⚠️ The list was fixed before searching, the minimum reliable source set was used, the pass stopped when the list was resolved, and no other item was pursued.

| Item verified | Where used | Status |
|---|---|---|
| The German invasion of Poland on 1 September 1939, and the British and French declarations of war on 3 September 1939 | Lesson 1 dependency map; Lesson 2; Map 1; register note 1 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account's chapter opens with Poland already invaded and does not date the declarations |
| The Lend-Lease Act signed on 11 March 1941, and the Atlantic Charter of 14 August 1941 | Lesson 11 section 2; Lesson 19 section 7; Map 1; register note 10 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ The core account refers to Lend-Lease on a page that is substantially damaged in the extraction and does not date the Charter in this chapter |
| The conference sequence: Casablanca, January 1943, at which unconditional surrender was announced; Tehran, 28 November to 1 December 1943; Yalta, 4-11 February 1945; and Potsdam, 17 July to 2 August 1945 | Lesson 11 section 5; Map 1; register note 10 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ The core account discusses unconditional surrender without naming or dating the conference at which it was announced, and does not date the later three in this chapter |
| Hitler's suicide on 30 April 1945; the German surrender signed at Reims on 7 May 1945 and ratified in Berlin on 8 May 1945 | Lesson 16 section 3; Map 1; register note 14 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ The core account records the suicide and the surrender without dating either |
| The Soviet declaration of war on Japan on 8 August 1945 and the invasion of Manchuria on 9 August 1945; the Japanese surrender announced on 15 August 1945 and signed on 2 September 1945 aboard the USS *Missouri* | Lesson 17 sections 2 and 4; Map 1; register note 15 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the compression of the nine-day window is the analytical point of Lesson 17 and the core account gives only the two bombing dates |
| The International Military Tribunal at Nuremberg opened on 20 November 1945; General Assembly Resolution 95 (I) was adopted on 11 December 1946 | Lesson 19 section 8, as the historical anchor of the single current linkage | ✅ Verified from the live source recorded below |
| The United Nations Charter as an institutional outcome | Identified and **deliberately not developed** | ⚠️ The institution is owned by the global-governance topic; this session states only that the UN emerged as the League's successor, which the core account itself records |

⚠️ **Items identified but deliberately not pursued:** the detailed operational history of any campaign beyond what the core account supplies; Allied signals intelligence and code-breaking organisations, which the core account does not hold beyond the broken Japanese radio code at Midway and which are therefore recorded as an open gap in Lesson 10; national war-production series; comparative rationing and food-supply data; women's war-work statistics for 1939-45; the Bengal famine of 1943; the internal administration of the camp system beyond what the core account records; and the named resistance movements excluded by the canonical owner's bounded-anchor rule. ⚠️ Each is recorded in the relevant lesson as a boundary or an open limitation rather than filled by inference.

## Evidence and limitation controls applied throughout

1. ⚠️ Quantities appear only where a permitted source records them, with the measure stated, and are never totalled, averaged or extrapolated. The principal permitted set is listed in the session opening, together with the catch-all rule covering the smaller counts used inside individual evidence sentences.
2. ⚠️ **No casualty total is asserted anywhere in this file** for the war, for any campaign, for any battle, for any bombing or for the atomic attacks. The single attributed estimate for the murder of Europe's Jews is the one permitted exception. It is **asserted at five points** - the declaration of permitted quantities in the session opening; the teaching statement in Lesson 13 section 2; Lesson 13 revision note 3; the corrected formulation of remediation item D3; and consolidated register note 11 - and it is **described at two further points inside this ledger**, in the book-evidence row and in the discrepancy table, where the competing formulations carried by the source itself are recorded. ⚠️ That is seven occurrences of the figure in total, five of them assertions and two of them provenance. ⚠️ At every one of the seven it is attributed and accompanied by the statement that estimates vary, and no cumulative concept check, Mains model answer or map reproduces it.
3. ⚠️ Other groups targeted by Nazi mass murder are named separately wherever the subject arises and are never folded into that estimate.
4. ⚠️ Persecution and occupation are taught through named instruments, institutions, ministries, units and dates, without atrocity description.
5. ⚠️ Responsibility for violence, occupation and genocide is attributed to governments, high commands, ministries, SS units, police organisations, party formations, courts, industrial firms and named individuals, never to nations, peoples or religions.
6. ⚠️ The one collective formulation held in the evidence base is reported as its author's recorded position, examined against the same evidence base and explicitly not adopted, with counter-evidence supplied from the same source.
7. ⚠️ Contested questions - the Balkan delay, the intentionalist-functionalist debate, the effectiveness and morality of strategic bombing, the causes of Japan's surrender, the reasons for German persistence in 1945 - are reported as contested with the positions named, and are resolved only where the evidence base itself resolves them.
8. ⚠️ Counterfactuals are used only where a permitted source licenses them, and each retains the source's own conditional wording.
9. ⚠️ Three extraction limitations are disclosed at the point of use rather than filled, in Lessons 11, 16 and 19, with a fourth noted in this ledger.
10. ⚠️ Two open gaps are recorded rather than filled: women's war-work data for 1939-45 in Lesson 14, and the Bengal famine of 1943 in Lesson 15. Allied signals intelligence beyond the broken Japanese code is recorded as a third in Lesson 10.
11. ⚠️ Boundary material belonging to the interwar-diplomacy, fascism, Russian Revolution, Cold War, United Nations and decolonisation owners is excluded, with each handover stated in ordinary teaching language.
12. ⚠️ No compiled objective-question corpus, key, rotation or option-elimination material appears anywhere in this file.
13. ⚠️ There is exactly one current linkage. It is taught once, in Lesson 19 section 8, and is otherwise only recorded - in that lesson's pre-teach checklist, in its revision-note item 13, and in the live-source row and source-manifest gate of this ledger. It is never extended, and no other lesson carries one.
14. ⚠️ Source-ownership, material-pass and retrieval machinery is confined to this ledger and to the coverage matrix, and does not appear as learner-facing teaching labelling.
